not share any symbols and that the
q_{i}
share only the symbols from

\Sigma
. We define
Q_{\Gamma}=(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
where

\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}_{1}\cup\cdots\cup\mathcal{T}_{n}
,
q
is the BUtCQ obtained
from
q_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge q_{n}
by pulling disjunction outside,
and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
are defined as expected. It is
then possible to prove an analogue of
Lemma
33
, we only sketch the required
modifications. In the reduction of
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma)^{\mathsf{sur}}
to
the evaluation problem for
Q_{\Gamma}
, one builds on ABox
\mathcal{A}
for
each
\mathcal{I}\in\Gamma
, each as in the corresponding of the proof of
Lemma
33
, and then takes their union.
In the reduction of the evaluation problem for
Q_{\Gamma}
to

\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma)^{\mathsf{sur}}
, one first checks whether for some
i
,
the given
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
contains an assertion
A_{i}(a)
, but
no assertion
V_{i}(a)
and answers ‘
\mathcal{A}\models Q_{\Gamma}
’ if this is
the case (this corresponds to Point (2) in the original proof). One
then checks whether for some
i
there is no assertion of the form

A_{i}(a)
and answers ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q_{\Gamma}
’ if this is
the case (corresponding to Point (1) in the original proof). Point (3)
and the remainder of the reduction need no major adaptations.

In summary, we have obtained the following result.

Theorem 34
.

For every
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma)^{\mathsf{sur}}
, there is an OMQC
Q_{\Gamma}
in

(\text{DL-Lite}_{\text{core}},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BUtCQ})
such that the
complement of
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma)^{\mathsf{sur}}
has the same complexity as
the evaluation problem for
Q_{\Gamma}
, up to polynomial time reductions. The same
holds for
(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BAQ})
.

We note that, as can easily be verified by checking the
constructions in the proof of Lemma
33
,
the complement of
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma)^{\mathsf{sur}}
and the evaluation problem
for
Q_{\Gamma}
actually have the same complexity up to
FO reductions

[
35
]
. This links the complexity of the two problems even
closer. For example, if one is complete for
LogSpace
or in
AC
0
, then so is the other.

We now establish a rather general converse of Theorem
34
by
showing that for every OMQC
Q
from

(\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BUtCQ})
, there is a generalized
surjective CSP that has the same complexity as the complement of the evaluation problem for
Q
, up to polynomial time
reductions.

Let
Q=(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
be an OMQC from

(\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BUtCQ})
. We can assume w.l.o.g. that
q
is a
BAQ, essentially because every tCQ can be rewritten into an
\mathcal{ALCI}
concept; see the remark on
\mathcal{EL}
and BAQs made after the proof of
Lemma
33
. Thus, let
q=\exists x\,A_{0}(x)

with
A_{0}
a concept name in
\mathcal{T}
.
We use the notation for types introduced in
Section
5
. A subset
T
of the set

{\sf TP}(\mathcal{T})
of
\mathcal{T}
-types is
realizable in a
countermodel of
Q
if there is a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
and model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}

such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
and

T=\{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\}
.
The desired surjective generalized CSP is defined by taking one template for each

T\subseteq\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})
that is realizable in a countermodel of
Q
.
The signature
\Sigma
of the CSP comprises the predicates
in
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
and one concept name
\overline{A}
for each concept name in
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. We assume
w.l.o.g. that there is at least one concept name in
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}

and at least one concept name
A_{\mathsf{open}}\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}\setminus\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.

Pick for every
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
an element
d_{A}
.
Then for each
T\subseteq\mathsf{TP}(\mathcal{T})
realizable in a countermodel of
Q

we define the template
\mathcal{I}_{T}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}&=&T\uplus\{d_{A}\mid A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}\}\\[2.84526pt]
A^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}&=&\{t\in T\mid A\in t\}\cup\{d_{B}\mid B\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}\setminus\{A\}\}\\[2.84526pt]
\overline{A}^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}&=&\{t\in T\mid A\notin t\}\cup\{d_{B}\mid B\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}\setminus\{A\}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}&=&\{(t,t^{\prime})\in T\times T\mid t\rightsquigarrow_{r}t^{\prime}\}\,\cup\\[2.84526pt]
&&\{(d,d^{\prime})\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}\mid\{d,d^{\prime}\}\setminus T\neq\emptyset\}.\end{array}

Note that, in
\mathcal{I}_{T}
restricted to domain
T
,
\overline{A}
is
interpreted as the complement of
A
. At each element
d_{A}
, all
concept names except
A
and
\overline{A}
are true, and these
elements are connected to all elements with all roles. Intuitively, we
need the concept names
\overline{A}
to ensure that when an assertion

A(a)
is missing in an ABox
\mathcal{A}
with
A
closed, then
a
can only
be mapped to a template element that does not make
A
true; this is
done by extending
\mathcal{A}
with
\overline{A}(a)
and exploiting that

\overline{A}
is essentially the complement of
A
in each

\mathcal{I}_{T}
. The elements
d_{A}
are then needed to deal with inputs to
the CSP where some point satisfies neither
A
nor
\overline{A}
.
Let
\Gamma_{Q}
be the set of all interpretations
\mathcal{I}_{T}
obtained in
the described way.

Lemma 35
.

Let
Q=(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
be an OMQC from

(\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BUtCQ})
. Then the evaluation problem for
Q
reduces in polynomial time to
the complement of
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma_{Q})^{\mathsf{sur}}
.

Proof 7.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
-ABox that is an input for
Q
and let

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be its extension with

(1)

all assertions
\overline{A}(a)
such that
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
a\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, and
A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}
;

(2)

assertions
A_{\mathsf{open}}(a_{B})
, where
a_{B}
is a fresh individual name
for each
B\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.

We claim that
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q
iff there is an interpretation

\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
such that there exists a surjective
homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. The assertions of
type (2) are needed to obtain a homomorphism that is surjective in
the ‘
\Rightarrow
’ direction, despite the presence of the elements

d_{B}
in the templates in
\Gamma_{Q}
.

(
\Leftarrow
). Let
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
and let
h
be a surjective
homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Note that each element
a
of

{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
is mapped by
h
to some element
t\in T
of
\mathcal{I}_{T}
because

A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
or
\overline{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
for every
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
(which is non-empty). Since
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
,
there are a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
-ABox
\mathcal{B}
and a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
and

\mathcal{B}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
such that

\mathcal{I}\not\models q
and
T=\{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})\}
.
For each
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, set
t_{a}=h(a)\in T
and for each

d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, set
t_{d}=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)
. Construct an
interpretation
\mathcal{J}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}&=&\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\cup(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B}))\\[2.84526pt]
A^{\mathcal{J}}&=&\{d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\mid A\in t_{d}\}\\[2.84526pt]
r^{\mathcal{J}}&=&\{(d,e)\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\times\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}\mid t_{d}\rightsquigarrow_{r}t_{e}\}.\end{array}

First note that
\mathcal{J}
is clearly a model of
\mathcal{A}
that respects
closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Specifically, if
A(a)\in\mathcal{A}
,
then
h(a)\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}
, thus
A\in h(a)=t_{a}
by construction
of
\mathcal{I}_{T}
which yields
a\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
by construction of
\mathcal{J}
; if

r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
, then
(h(a),h(b))\in r^{\mathcal{I}_{T}}
, thus
t_{a}\rightsquigarrow_{r}t_{b}
implying
(a,b)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
; finally if
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
, then we must have
d=a
for
some
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
by definition of
\mathcal{J}
and since
d\notin A^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})
.
Thus,
A\in t_{a}=h(a)
by construction of
\mathcal{J}
. This implies
A(a)\in\mathcal{A}
since otherwise
\overline{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, which would
imply
\overline{A}\in h(a)
, in contradiction to
A\in h(a)
.

It thus remains to show that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{J}\not\models q
. By definition,
\mathcal{J}
satisfies all RIs in

\mathcal{T}
. Satisfaction of the CIs in
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{J}\not\models q
follow from the subsequent claim together with the condition that no type in
T
contains
A_{0}
and each type in
\mathcal{I}_{T}
is satisfied in a model of
\mathcal{T}
.

Claim
.

For all
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
C\in\mathsf{cl}(\mathcal{T})
, we have
d\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
iff
C\in t_{d}
.

Proof 7.3

(Proof of claim.)
.

The proof is by induction on the structure of
C
, with the
induction start and the cases
C=\neg D
and
C=D_{1}\sqcap D_{2}

being trivial. Thus let
C=\exists r.D
and first assume
d\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
. Then there is an
e\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
with
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
. Thus
t_{d}\rightsquigarrow_{r}t_{e}
by definition of
\mathcal{J}
,
and IH yields
D\in t_{e}
. By definition of ‘
\rightsquigarrow_{r}
’,
we must thus have
C\in t_{d}
as required. Now let
C\in t_{d}
. We
distinguish two cases:

•

d=a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
.

Let
a^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})
be such that

h(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\prime})
. Since
t_{a}=h(a)
, we must have
a^{\prime}\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
and thus there is some
e\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
with
(a^{\prime},e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
, which yields
\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\prime})\rightsquigarrow_{r}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(e)
and
D\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(e)
. If
e=b^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})
, then since
h
is surjective there is some
b\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
with
h(b)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(b^{\prime})
. We have

t_{a}=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a^{\prime})
and
t_{b}=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(b^{\prime})
, thus
t_{a}\rightsquigarrow_{r}t_{b}
which yields
(a,b)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
by
definition of
\mathcal{J}
. We also have
D\in t_{b}
, which by IH yields

b\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

•

d\notin\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
.

Then
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})
. Since
C\in t_{d}
, we thus have
C\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)
. Thus, there is an
e\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
with
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
, which implies

\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)\rightsquigarrow_{r}\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(e)
and
D\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(e)
. If
e\notin\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})
, then the
definition of
\mathcal{J}
and IH yields
d\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
. Thus assume
e=b^{\prime}\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})
. Since
h
is surjective, there is some
b\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
with
h(b)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(b^{\prime})
. Since

t_{d}=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(d)
and
t_{b}=h(b)
, we have
t_{d}\rightsquigarrow_{r}t_{b}
, thus
(d,b)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
. By IH,
D\in\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(b^{\prime})=h(b)
yields
b\in D^{\mathcal{J}}
.

(\Rightarrow)
. Assume that
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q
. Then there is a
model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed predicates

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
be the corresponding template, that is,
T=\{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\}
. For each
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, set
h(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)
; for each
a_{B}\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, set
h(a_{B})=d_{B}
(recall that such
a_{B}
have
been added to
{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
for every
B\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
). It is
readily checked that
h
is a surjective homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}}

to
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. In particular,
\overline{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
implies

A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, thus
A\notin\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)
(since
A
is
closed), which yields
h(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\in\overline{A}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}}}
by definition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Lemma 36
.

Let
Q=(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
be an OMQC from

(\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BUtCQ})
. Then
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma_{Q})^{\mathsf{sur}}

reduces in polynomial time to the complement of the evaluation problem for
Q
.

Proof 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
be the ABox corresponding to an input
\mathcal{J}
for
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma_{Q})^{\mathsf{sur}}
. An
element
a
of
{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
is
special for

A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
if

A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\overline{A}(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
; it is

special
if it is special for some
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.
First perform the following checks:

(1)

if there is a non-special element
a
of
{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})
such that

A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\overline{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
for some
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
, then return ‘no’ (there is no template in
\Gamma_{Q}

that has any element to which
a
can be mapped by a
homomorphism);

(2)

if
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
does not contain a family of distinct elements

(a_{A})_{A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}
, such that each
a_{A}
is special for

A
, then return ‘no’ (we cannot map surjectively to the elements

d_{A}
of the templates in
\Gamma_{Q}
).

Note that, to check Condition 2, we can go through all candidate
families in polytime since the size of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
is constant.
If none of the above checks succeeds, then let
\mathcal{A}
be the ABox
obtained from
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
by

•

deleting all assertions of the form
\overline{A}(a)
and

•

deleting all special elements.

We have to show that
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q
iff there exists an
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
such that there is a surjective homomorphism from
\mathcal{J}

to
\mathcal{I}_{T}
.

(
\Leftarrow
). Let
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
and let
h
be a surjective
homomorphism from
\mathcal{J}
to
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Note that each element
a
of

{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
is mapped by
h
to some element
t\in T
of
\mathcal{I}_{T}
because

A(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
or
\overline{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
for every
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
(which is non-empty). Since
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
,
there is a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
-ABox
\mathcal{B}
and model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
and

\mathcal{B}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and such that

\mathcal{I}\not\models q
and
T=\{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{B})\}
. We can now proceed as in the proof of
Lemma
35
to build a model
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that
respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and such that
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\not\models q
.

(
\Rightarrow
). Assume that
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q
. Then there is a model
\mathcal{I}
of

\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{T}\in\Gamma_{Q}
be the
corresponding template, that is,
T=\{\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\}
. For each
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, set

h(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)
; for each element
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime})\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, we can choose some
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}

such that
A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\overline{A}(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, and
set
h(a)=d_{A}
; by Check 2 above, these choices can be made such
that the resulting map
h
is surjective. Moreover, it is readily
checked that
h
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{J}
to
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. In
particular,
\overline{A}(a)\in\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
implies
A(a)\notin\mathcal{A}^{\prime}

by Check 1, thus
A\notin\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)
(since
A
is closed),
which yields
h(a)=\mathsf{tp}_{\mathcal{I}}(a)\in\overline{A}^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}}}
by
definition of
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}}
.

We have thus established the following result.

Theorem 37
.

For every OMQC
Q
from
(\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI},{\sf N_{C}},\text{BUtCQ})
, there
is a generalized
\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma_{Q})^{\mathsf{sur}}

such that the evaluation problem for
Q
has the same complexity as the complement of

\mathsf{CSP}(\Gamma_{Q})^{\mathsf{sur}}
, up to polynomial time reductions.

Again, the theorem can easily be strengthened to state the same
complexity up to FO reductions. Note that the DL
\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI}
used in Theorem
37
is a significant extension of the
DLs referred to in Theorem
34
and thus our results
apply to a remarkable range of DLs: all DLs between

\text{DL-Lite}_{\text{core}}
and
\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI}
as well as all DLs between

\mathcal{EL}
and
\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI}
.

8.
Closing Role Names in the Fixed Query Case: Turing Machine Equivalence

We generalize the setup from the previous section by allowing also
role names to be closed. Our main results are that for every
non-determinstic polynomial time Turing machine
M
, there is an OMQC

Q
in
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
such that evaluating
Q

and the complement of
M
’s word problem are polynomial time reducible
to each other, and that it is undecidable whether evaluating OMQCs in

(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
is in
PTime
(unless
PTime
=

NP
). By Ladner’s theorem, it follows that there are

coNP
-intermediate OMQCs (unless
PTime
=
NP
) and that a full
complexity classification of the OMQCs in this language is beyond
reach of the techniques available today. As in the previous section,
the same results hold for
(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BAQ})
.

To establish these results, we utilize two related results
from
[
47
,
14
]
:
(1) for every
NP
Turing machine
M
, there is an ontology-mediated
query
Q
from
(\mathcal{ALCF},\emptyset,\text{BAQ})
such that
evaluating
Q
is reducible in polynomial time to the complement of

M
’s word problem and vice versa, where
\mathcal{ALCF}
is the
extension of
\mathcal{ALC}
with functional roles; and (2) it is
undecidable whether an OMQC from

(\mathcal{ALCF},\emptyset,\text{BAQ})
is in
PTime
. For using these
results in our context, however, it is more convenient to phrase them
in terms of (a certain kind of) monadic disjunctive datalog programs
with inequality rather than in terms of OMQCs from

(\mathcal{ALCF},\emptyset,\text{BAQ})
. This is what we do in the
following, starting with the introduction of a suitable version of
monadic disjunctive datalog. For a more thorough introduction, see
[
25
]
.

A
monadic disjunctive datalog rule (MDD
rule)

\rho
takes the form

P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{m}(x)\leftarrow R_{1}(\vec{x}_{1})\land\cdots\land R_{n}(\vec{x}_{n})\quad\mbox{ or }\quad{\sf goal}\leftarrow R_{1}(\vec{x}_{1})\land\cdots\land R_{n}(\vec{x}_{n})

with
m,n>0
and where all
P_{i}
are unary predicates,
{\sf goal}
is the
goal predicate
of arity
0
,
and all
R_{i}
are predicates of arity one or two, including possibly the non-equality predicate
\not=
.
We refer to
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{m}(x)
and, respectively,
{\sf goal}
as the
head
of
\rho
, and to
R_{1}(\vec{x}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge R_{n}(\vec{x}_{n})
as the
body
. A
monadic disjunctive
datalog (MDD) program

\Pi
is a finite set of MDD rules containing at least one rule with the goal predicate in its head and
no rule with the goal predicate in its body. Predicates that occur in the head of at
least one rule of
\Pi
are
intensional (IDB) predicates
, denoted
{\sf IDB}(\Pi)
, and
all remaining predicates in
\Pi
are
extensional (EDB) predicates
, denoted
{\sf EDB}(\Pi)
.
An interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a
model
of
\Pi
if it satisfies all rules in
\Pi
(viewed as universally quantified first-order sentences).

\Pi
is
entailed on
a
{\sf EDB}(\Pi)
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
, in symbols
\mathcal{A}\models\Pi
, iff
{\sf goal}
is true in every model of
\Pi
and
\mathcal{A}
.
Note that it suffices to consider models that respect closed predicates
{\sf EDB}(\Pi)
.
The
evaluation problem for
\Pi
is the problem to decide whether
\Pi
is entailed by an
{\sf EDB}(\Pi)
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
.

For our reduction, we use the following kind of MDD programs that we
call basic. A binary predicate
r
is
functional in an ABox

\mathcal{A}
if
r(a,b_{1}),r(a,b_{2})\in\mathcal{A}
implies
b_{1}=b_{2}
and

r
is
empty in

\mathcal{A}
if
r
does not occur in
\mathcal{A}
. Then an
MDD program
\Pi
is
basic
if

•

\Pi
uses exactly two binary predicates,
r_{1},r_{2}
, and
contains exactly the following functionality rules, for
i=1,2
:

{\sf goal}\leftarrow r_{i}(x,y)\wedge r_{i}(x,z)\wedge(y\not=z)

•

all remaining rules of
\Pi
are of the form

P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\quad\mbox{ or }\quad{\sf goal}\leftarrow q

where
n\geq 1
and
q
is a dtCQ with root
x
(with the quantifier prefix removed).

•

if
r_{1},r_{2}
are functional and at least one
r_{i}
is empty in an
{\sf EDB}(\Pi)
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{A}\not\models\Pi
.

The following result can be obtained by starting from the results for

(\mathcal{ALCF},\emptyset,\text{BAQ})
from

[
47
,
14
]

mentioned above and translating the involved OMQs into a basic MDD
program. Such a translation is given in

[
14
]
for the case of
\mathcal{ALC}
TBoxes
and MDD programs without inequality, but the extension to functional
roles and inequality is trivial.

Theorem 38
.

(1)

For every non-deterministic polynomial time Turing machine
M
, there exists a basic MDD program
\Pi
such that the
evaluation problem for
\Pi
and the complement of
M
’s word problem are polynomial time reducible to each other.

(2)

It is undecidable whether the evaluation problem for a basic MDD program is in
PTime
(unless
PTime
=
NP
).

We next prove the following central theorem.

Theorem 39
.

For every basic MDD program
\Pi
, one can construct an
OMQC
Q
in the language
(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BAQ})
such that the evaluation problem for
Q

and
\Pi
are polynomial time reducible to each other. The same is true for
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
.

Proof 8.1
.

Assume a basic MDD program
\Pi
of the form defined above is given. We first construct an OMQC
Q_{\Pi}=(\mathcal{T}_{\Pi},\Sigma_{\Pi},\Sigma_{\Pi},q_{\Pi})
in

(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
and then obtain the
required OMQCs in
(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BAQ})
and
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
by rather straightforward
modifications of
Q_{\Pi}
. Note that we construct a
Q_{\Pi}
in which the ABox signature and set of closed predicates coincide.
We set
\Sigma_{\Pi}={\sf EDB}(\Pi)\cup\{T,F,V\}
, where
T,F,V
are fresh concept names. We also use auxiliary predicates which are
not in the ABox signature of
Q_{\Pi}
: role names
{\sf val}_{P}
for every unary
P\in{\sf IDB}(\Pi)
and role names
s_{i}

and concept names
A_{i},B_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
contains the following CIs:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}T&\sqsubseteq&V\\[2.84526pt]
F&\sqsubseteq&V\\[2.84526pt]
\top&\sqsubseteq&\exists{\sf val}_{P}.V,\text{ for all unary $P\in{\sf IDB}(\Pi)$ }\\[2.84526pt]
\top&\sqsubseteq&\exists s_{i}.(\exists r_{i}.A_{i}\sqcap\exists r_{i}.B_{i}),\text{ for $i=1,2$ }.\end{array}

Using
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
, we encode the truth value of IDB predicates
P
using the CQs

P^{T}(x,y):=({\sf val}_{P}(x,y)\wedge T(y)),\quad P^{F}(x,y):=({\sf val}_{P}(x,y)\wedge F(y)).

For any tCQ
q
, we denote by
q^{T}
the result of replacing every occurrence of an
IDB
P(x)
in
q
by
P^{T}(x,y_{0})
, where the variable
y_{0}
is fresh for every
occurrence of
P(x)
, and existentially quantified.
Thus,
q^{T}
is again a tCQ (and a dtCQ if
q
is already a dtCQ).
The final CI is used to encode functionality of the roles
r_{1},r_{2}
. We define
CQs
q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
and
q_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
by setting

q_{\mathcal{F}}^{i}=(s_{i}(x,y)\wedge r_{i}(y,z)\wedge A_{i}(z)\wedge B_{i}(z)),

for
i=1,2
. Then, for
the OMQC
Q_{i}=(\{\top\sqsubseteq\exists s_{i}.(\exists r_{i}.A_{i}\sqcap\exists r_{i}.B_{i})\},\Sigma_{\Pi},\Sigma_{\Pi},\exists y\,\exists z\,q_{\mathcal{F}}^{i})

and any
\Sigma_{\Pi}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
:

•

if
r_{i}
is empty in
\mathcal{A}
, then
\mathcal{A}
is not consistent w.r.t.
(\{\top\sqsubseteq\exists s_{i}.(\exists r_{i}.A_{i}\sqcap\exists r_{i}.B_{i})\},\Sigma_{\Pi})
, and

•

if
r_{i}
is not empty in
\mathcal{A}
, then
r_{i}
is functional in
\mathcal{A}
iff

\mathcal{A}\models Q_{i}(a)
, for some (equivalently, all)
a\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
.

Define
q_{\Pi}
as the union of the following Boolean CQs, where for brevity we omit the existential quantifiers:

•

q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\wedge q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\wedge q^{T}
, for every rule

{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi
, where we assume that the only variable shared by
any two of the conjuncts
q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
,
q_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
and
q^{T}
is
x
.

•

q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\wedge q_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}\wedge q^{T}\wedge\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}P_{i}^{F}
,
for every
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi
,
where we assume again that the only variable shared by any two of the conjuncts
q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}
,

q_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}
,
q^{T}
,
P_{i}^{F}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, is
x
.

We prove the following

Claim
.

The problem of evaluating
\Pi
and the problem of evaluating
Q_{\Pi}
are polynomial time reducible to each other.

Proof 8.2

(Proof of claim.)
.

(
\Rightarrow
) Assume an
\mathsf{EDB}(\Pi)
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
is given
as an input to
\Pi
. If
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
is not functional in
\mathcal{A}
, then output
‘
\mathcal{A}\models\Pi
’. Otherwise, if
r_{1}
or
r_{2}
is empty, then output ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models\Pi
’.
Now assume that
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are not empty and both are functional in
\mathcal{A}
.
Let

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}\cup\{T(a),F(b),V(a),V(b)\},

where we asume w.l.o.g. that
a,b
occur in
{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. We show that

\mathcal{A}\models\Pi
iff
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\models Q_{\Pi}
.

Assume first that
\mathcal{A}\not\models\Pi
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of

\mathcal{A}
and
\Pi
that respects closed predicates
\mathsf{EDB}(\Pi)
and
satisfies no body of any rule
{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi
. Define
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
in the same
way as
\mathcal{I}
except that

•

T^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a\}
,
F^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{b\}
, and
V^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{a,b\}
;

•

s_{i}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times{\sf dom}(r_{i}^{\mathcal{I}})
and
A_{i}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=B_{i}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, for
i=1,2
,
where
{\sf dom}(r^{\mathcal{I}})
denotes the domain of
r^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

{\sf val}_{P}^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=(P^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{a\})\cup((\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus P^{\mathcal{I}})\times\{b\})
,
for all unary
P\in{\sf IDB}(\Pi)
.

It is straightforward to show that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}

and
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\Pi}
. It
remains to show that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\not\models q_{\Pi}
. To this end it is
sufficient to show that

(1)

No
q^{T}
with
{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi
is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
;

(2)

No
q^{T}\wedge\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}P_{i}^{F}
with
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi

is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.

Point (1) holds since
P^{\mathcal{I}}=\{d\mid\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\models\exists y\,P^{T}(d,y))\}
for all unary
P\in{\sf IDB}(\Pi)
,
by definition of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and since
q
is not satisfied in
\mathcal{I}
for any rule
{\sf goal}()\leftarrow q\in\Pi
.
Point (2) holds since all rules
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi
are satisfied in
\mathcal{I}

and
P^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\{d\mid\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\models\exists y\,P^{F}(d,y)\}
for all unary
P\in{\sf IDB}(\Pi)
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\not\models Q_{\Pi}
. Take a model

\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\Pi}
and
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q_{\Pi}
. Define a model
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
by modifying
\mathcal{I}
by setting

P^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=\{d\mid\mathcal{I}\models\exists y\,P^{T}(d,y)\}
, for all unary
P\in{\sf IDB}(\Pi)
. It follows
from the condition that
r_{1},r_{2}
are non-empty and
functional in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
that
\mathcal{I}\models\forall x(\exists y\exists zq_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\wedge\exists y\exists zq_{\mathcal{F}}^{2})
.
From
\mathcal{I}\not\models q_{\Pi}
we obtain that no
q
with
{\sf goal}()\leftarrow q\in\Pi
is satisfied
in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and that all rules
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi
are satisfied in
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
and
\Pi
witnessing that
\mathcal{A}\not\models\Pi
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assume a
\Sigma_{\Pi}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
is given as an
input to
Q_{\Pi}
. There exists a model of
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
and
\mathcal{A}
that
respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\Pi}
iff (i)
V
is
non-empty in
\mathcal{A}
, (ii)
T,F
are both contained
in
V
in
\mathcal{A}
, and (iii)
r_{1},r_{2}
are
non-empty in
\mathcal{A}
. Thus, output ‘
\mathcal{A}\models Q_{\Pi}
’ whenever (i),
(ii), or (iii) is violated. Now assume (i), (ii), and (iii) hold. If

r_{1}
or
r_{2}
are not functional in
\mathcal{A}
, then we can construct a model of
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects
closed predicates
\Sigma_{\Pi}
and such that
\exists x(\exists y\exists zq_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\wedge\exists y\exists zq_{\mathcal{F}}^{2})
is not satisfied in
\mathcal{I}
. Hence, we output ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q_{\Pi}
’.
Thus, assume in addition to (i), (ii) and (iii) that
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
are functional in
\mathcal{A}
.
We distinguish five cases.
We only consider the first case in detail, the remaining cases are proved similarly.

(1)

If
F^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\cup T^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\not=V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, then output ‘
\mathcal{A}\models Q_{\Pi}
’ if there exists a rule
{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi
such that

q
contains not IDBs and
q
(which then equals
q^{T}
) is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
.
This is clearly correct since
\mathcal{A}\models(\mathcal{T}_{\Pi},\Sigma_{\Pi},\Sigma_{\Pi},q_{\mathcal{F}}^{1}\wedge q_{\mathcal{F}}^{2}\wedge q^{T})

follows.
Otherwise output ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q_{\Pi}
’. To prove correctness, let
a\in V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\setminus(F^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\cup T^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}})
. Construct a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects
\Sigma_{\Pi}

by extending
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
by setting
{\sf val}_{P}^{\mathcal{I}}=\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\times\{a\}
for all unary IDB predicates
P

and defining
s_{i}^{\mathcal{I}},A_{i}^{\mathcal{I}},B_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
,
i=1,2
, arbitrarily so that
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
is satisfied.
Then no
q^{T}
with
{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi
and no

\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}P_{i}^{F}
with
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi
is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}
.
Thus
\mathcal{I}\not\models q_{\Pi}
.

(2)

If
T^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}=F^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}=V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, then output ‘
\mathcal{A}\models Q_{\Pi}
’ if there exists a rule
{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi

or
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi
such that
q^{\prime}
is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
for the query
q^{\prime}
obtained from

q
by removing every atom
P(y)
from
q
with
P
a unary IDB. Otherwise output ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q_{\Pi}
’.

(3)

If
T^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}=V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
and
F^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\not=V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, then output ‘
\mathcal{A}\models Q_{\Pi}
’ if there exists a rule
{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi

such that
q^{\prime}
is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
for the query
q^{\prime}
obtained from
q
by removing every atom
P(y)
from
q
with
P
a unary IDB.
Otherwise output ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q_{\Pi}
’.

(4)

If
F^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}=V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
and
T^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\not=V^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}
, then output ‘
\mathcal{A}\models Q_{\Pi}
’ if there exists a rule

{\sf goal}\leftarrow q\in\Pi
or
P_{1}(x)\vee\cdots\vee P_{n}(x)\leftarrow q\in\Pi
such that
q
does not contain
any IDB and
q
is satisfied in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Otherwise output ‘
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q_{\Pi}
’.

(5)

If none of the four cases above apply, obtain
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
from
\mathcal{A}
by removing all assertions using
T,F
, or
V
.
Then
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\models\Pi
iff
\mathcal{A}\models Q
, and we have established the polynomial time reduction.

The modification of
Q_{\Pi}
needed to obtain an OMQC from

(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BAQ})
is the same as in the proof of Theorem
34
:
the query
q_{\Pi}
is a BUdtCQ and so we can replace it with a query of the form
\exists x\,A(x)
:
as the disjuncts of
q_{\Pi}
are of the form
\exists x\,q^{\prime}(x)
with
q^{\prime}(x)
a dtCQ, we can take the

\mathcal{EL}
concepts
C_{q^{\prime}}
corresponding to
q^{\prime}(x)
and extend
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
with
C_{q^{\prime}}\sqsubseteq A
for every such disjunct
\exists x\,q^{\prime}(x)
of
q
.

It remains to show how one can modify
Q_{\Pi}
to obtain an equivalent OMQC
Q_{\Pi}^{\prime}
from the language

(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
. First,
to eliminate
\top
on the left-hand-side of CIs in
\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}
, we replace
each CI
\top\sqsubseteq C
by the CIs
A\sqsubseteq C
,
\exists r\sqsubseteq C
, and
\exists r^{-}\sqsubseteq C
for any concept name

A\in\Sigma_{\Pi}
and role name
r\in\Sigma_{\Pi}
. Second, we
employ the standard encoding of qualified existential restrictions in

\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
by replacing exhaustively any
B\sqsubseteq\exists r.D
by
B\sqsubseteq\exists s
,
\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq A_{D}
,
A_{D}\sqsubseteq D
, and
s\sqsubseteq r
, where
A_{D}
is
a fresh concept name and
s
is a fresh role name. Let

\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}^{\prime}
be the resulting TBox. Then
Q_{\Pi}^{\prime}=(\mathcal{T}_{\Pi}^{\prime},\Sigma_{\Pi},\Sigma_{\Pi},q_{\Pi})
is as required.

From Theorems
38
and
39
, we obtain the main result of this section.

Theorem 40
.

(1)

For every non-deterministic polynomial time Turing machine
M
one can construct a OMQC
Q
in the languages

(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BAQ})
and
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})

such that the evaluation problem for
Q
and
M
’s word problem are polynomial time reducible to each other.

(2)

It is undecidable whether the evaluation problem for OMQCs in

(\mathcal{EL},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BAQ})
and
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
is in
PTime
(unless
PTime
=
NP
).

Note that Theorem
40
does not cover
\text{$\text{DL-Lite}$ }_{\text{core}}
. In fact,
the computational status of the language
(\text{$\text{DL-Lite}$ }_{\text{core}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})
remains open, and in particular it remains open whether
Theorem
40
can be strengthened to this case.

9.
Quantifier-Free UCQs and FO-Rewritability

The results in the previous sections have shown that intractability
comes quickly when predicates are closed. The aim of this section is
to identify a useful OMQC language whose UCQs are guaranteed to be
FO-rewritable. It turns out that one can obtain such a language by
combining
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
with quantifier-free UCQs, that is, unions of
quantifier-free CQs; we denote this class of queries with UqfCQ. Our
main result is that all OMQCs from the language

(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{UqfCQ})
are FO-rewritable under the
mild restriction that there is no RI which requires an open role to be
contained in a closed one. We believe that this class of OMQCs is
potentially relevant for practical applications. Note that the query
language SPARQL, which is used in many web applications, is closely
related to UqfCQs and, in fact, does not admit existential
quantification under its standard entailment regimes

[
30
]
. We also prove that the restriction
on RIs is needed for tractability, by constructing a
coNP
-hard OMQC
in
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{UqfCQ})
.

Theorem 41
.

Every OMQC
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
from

(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{UqfCQ})
such that

\mathcal{T}
contains no RI of the form
s\sqsubseteq r

with
{\sf sig}(s)\not\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\sf sig}(r)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
is
FO-rewritable.

We first show that ABox consistency w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
is FO-rewritable, for every

\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
TBox
\mathcal{T}
not containing any RI of the form
s\sqsubseteq r

with
{\sf sig}(s)\not\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and
{\sf sig}(r)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.
We make use of Theorem
1
and assume w.l.o.g. that

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
.
Let
\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
be the set of all concept names in
\mathcal{T}
, and all concepts

\exists r,\exists r^{-}
such that
r
is a
role name that occurs in
\mathcal{T}
. A
\mathcal{T}
-type
is a set
t\subseteq\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
such that for all
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
:

•

if
B_{1}\in t
and
\mathcal{T}\models B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2}
, then
B_{2}\in t
;

•

if
B_{1}\in t
and
\mathcal{T}\models B_{1}\sqsubseteq\neg B_{2}
, then
B_{2}\notin t
.

A
\mathcal{T}
-typing
is a set
T
of
\mathcal{T}
-types.
A
path in

T
is a sequence
t,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
where
t\in T
,

\exists r_{1},\dots,\exists r_{n}\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
use no
predicates from
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
\exists r_{1}\in t
and for

i\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
,
\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{i}^{-}\sqsubseteq\exists r_{i+1}
and
r_{i}^{-}\neq r_{i+1}
. The path is

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating
if for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
,
there is no
B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
with
\mathsf{sig}(B)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{i}^{-}\sqsubseteq B
while there is such a
B

for
i=n
. A
\mathcal{T}
-typing
T
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable
if for
every
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating path
t,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
in
T
, there is
some
u\in T
such that
\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists r^{-}_{n}\sqsubseteq B\}\subseteq u
.

A
\mathcal{T}
-typing
T
provides partial information about a model
\mathcal{I}
of

\mathcal{T}
and a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
by taking
T
to contain the
types that are realized in
\mathcal{I}
by ABox
elements.
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizability then ensures that we can build
from
T
a model that respects the closed predicates
in
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. To make this more precise, define a

\mathcal{T}
-decoration of
a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
to be a
mapping
f
that assigns to each
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
a
\mathcal{T}
-type

f(a)
such that
f(a)|_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}=t^{a}_{\mathcal{A}}|_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}

where
t^{a}_{\mathcal{A}}=\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid a\in B^{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}}\}
and

S|_{\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}
denotes the restriction of the set
S
of
concepts to those members that only use predicates
from
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.
The following lemma is proved in the appendix.

Lemma 42
.

A
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
is consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
iff

(1)

\mathcal{A}
has a
\mathcal{T}
-decoration
f
whose image is a

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable
\mathcal{T}
-typing and

(2)

if
s(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
, and
\mathsf{sig}(s\sqsubseteq r)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
then
r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
.

We now construct the required FOQ. For all role names
r
and
variables
x,y
, define
\psi_{r}(x,y)=r(x,y)
and

\psi_{r^{-}}(x,y)=r(y,x)
. For all concept names
A
and roles
r
,
define
\psi_{A}(x)=A(x)
and
\psi_{\exists r}(x)=\exists y\,\psi_{r}(x,y)
. For each
\mathcal{T}
-type
t
, set

\psi_{t}(x)=\hskip-14.22636pt\bigwedge_{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\setminus t\text{ with
}\mathsf{sig}(B)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}\neg\psi_{B}(x)\land\hskip-11.38109pt\bigwedge_{B\in t\text{ with }\mathsf{sig}(B)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}\psi_{B}(x)

and for each
\mathcal{T}
-typing
T=\{t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\}
, set

\psi_{T}=\forall x\bigvee_{t\in T}\psi_{t}(x)\land\exists x_{1}\cdots\exists x_{n}(\bigwedge_{i\neq j}x_{i}\neq x_{j}\land\bigwedge_{i}\psi_{t_{i}}(x_{i})).

Let
\mathcal{R}
be the set of all
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable typings and set

\Psi_{\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}=\bigvee_{T\in\mathcal{R}}\psi_{T}\wedge\bigwedge_{\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r,\mathsf{sig}(s\sqsubseteq r)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}\forall x\forall y(\psi_{s}(x,y)\rightarrow\psi_{r}(x,y)).

Note that the two conjuncts of
\Psi_{\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}
express exactly
Points (1) and (2) of Lemma
42
. We have thus shown
the following.

Proposition 43
.

A
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
is consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
iff

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\models\Psi_{\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}
.

The next step is to construct an FO-rewriting of

Q=(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
over
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABoxes that are
consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
. Whereas the FO-rewriting
\Psi_{\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}
above is Boolean and
identifies ABoxes that have a common model with
\mathcal{T}
respecting closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
, we now aim to
construct a FOQ
\Phi_{Q}(\vec{x})
such that for all

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABoxes
\mathcal{A}
consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
and
\vec{a}\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, we have

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\models\Phi_{Q}(\vec{a})
iff
\mathcal{A}\models Q(\vec{a})
. The desired FO-rewriting
of
Q
is then constructed as
\neg\Psi_{\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}}\vee\Phi_{Q}(\vec{x})
.
The construction of
\Phi_{Q}(\vec{x})
is
based on an extended notion of
\mathcal{T}
-typing called

(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
that provides partial information about a
model
\mathcal{I}
of

\mathcal{T}
and a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
respecting
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
which avoids an assignment from

\vec{x}
to certain individual names
\vec{a}
.

Let
q=\bigvee_{i\in I}q_{i}
with answer variables

\vec{x}=x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
A
(\mathcal{T},q)
-
typing

T
is a quadruple

(\sim,f_{0},\Gamma,\Delta)
where

•

\sim
is an equivalence relation on
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
;

•

f_{0}
is a function that assigns a
\mathcal{T}
-type
f_{0}(x_{i})

to each
x_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, such that

f_{0}(x_{i})=f_{0}(x_{j})
when
x_{i}\sim x_{j}
;

•

\Gamma
is a
\mathcal{T}
-typing;

•

\Delta
is a set of atoms
s(x_{i},x_{j})
,
s\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
such that
s(x_{i},x_{j})\in\Delta
iff
s(x_{i}^{\prime},x_{j}^{\prime})\in\Delta
when
x_{i}\sim x_{i}^{\prime}
and
x_{j}\sim x_{j}^{\prime}
.

Intuitively,
\sim
describes the answer variables that are
identified by an assignment
\pi
for
q
in an ABox
\mathcal{A}
,
f_{0}(x_{i})

describes the
\mathcal{T}
-type of the ABox individual name
\pi(x_{i})
,

\Gamma
describes the
\mathcal{T}
-types of ABox individual names that are not
in the range of
\pi
, and
\Delta
fixes role relationships that do

not
hold between the
\pi(x_{i})
. Let
X
be a set of atoms.
Then
T

avoids
X
if the following conditions hold:

1. for all
x_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, if
A\in f_{0}(x_{i})
, then
A(x_{i})\not\in X
;

2. for all
x_{i}
,
1\leq i\leq n
, if
\exists s\in f_{0}(x_{i})
, then for
S=\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}
the following holds: (i)
S
contains no predicate
from
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
or (ii) there is a
u\in\Gamma
such that

S\subseteq u
or (iii) there is a
y
such that
S\subseteq f_{0}(y)
and there are no
x^{\prime}\sim x_{i}
and
y^{\prime}\sim y
such that

r(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in X
and
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
, or

r(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})\in X
and
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r^{-}
;

3. if
r(x,y)\in X
, then
\Delta
contains all
s(x,y)
with
s\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
and all
s(y,x)
with

s\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and
\mathcal{T}\models s^{-}\sqsubseteq r
.

T

avoids

q
if it avoids some set
X
of
atoms containing an atom
\alpha_{i}
in
q_{i}
for any
i\in I
.
We use
\mathsf{tp}(T)
to denote the
\mathcal{T}
-typing
\Gamma
extended with all

\mathcal{T}
-types in the range of
f_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
and let
\pi
assign individual names
\pi(x_{i})
to
x_{i}
,

1\leq i\leq n
, such that
\pi(x_{i})=\pi(x_{j})
iff
x_{i}\sim x_{j}
. A
\mathcal{T}
-decoration
f
of
\mathcal{A}

realizes

T=(\sim,f_{0},\Gamma,\Delta)

using

\pi
iff
\mathsf{tp}(T)

is the range of
f
,
f_{0}(x_{i})=f(\pi(x_{i}))
for
1\leq i\leq n
, and
r(\pi(x_{i}),\pi(x_{j}))\not\in\mathcal{A}
if
r(x_{i},x_{j})\in\Delta
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
and all
r\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.
\mathcal{A}

realizes

T
using
\pi
if there exists a
\mathcal{T}
-decoration

f
that realizes
T
using
\pi
.

Lemma 44
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox consistent
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
. Then
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
iff
\mathcal{A}
realizes some

(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
T
using
\pi
that avoids
q
and such that

\mathsf{tp}(T)
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable.

The proof is a modification of the proof of Lemma
42
and given in the
appendix.

We now construct the actual rewriting
\Phi_{Q}(\vec{x})
. For every

(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
T=(\sim,f_{0},\Gamma,\Delta)
with

\Gamma=\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\}

let
\Psi_{T}(\vec{x})
be the conjunction of the following:

\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}\psi_{f_{0}(x_{i})}(x_{i})\wedge\bigwedge_{x_{i}\sim x_{j}}(x_{i}=x_{j})\wedge\bigwedge_{x_{i}\not\sim x_{j}}(x_{i}\not=x_{j})

\bigwedge_{r(x_{i},x_{j})\in\Delta}\neg r(x_{i},x_{j})\wedge\forall y(\bigwedge_{1\leq i\leq n}(y\not=x_{i})\rightarrow\bigvee_{t\in\Gamma}\psi_{t}(y))

\exists y_{1}\cdots\exists y_{k}(\bigwedge_{j\neq i}y_{j}\neq y_{i}\wedge\bigwedge_{j\leq k,i\leq n}x_{i}\neq y_{j}\wedge\bigwedge_{j\leq k}\psi_{t_{j}}(y_{j}))

Then
\Phi_{Q}(\vec{x})
is the conjunction over all
\neg\Psi_{T}(\vec{x})
such that
T
avoids
q
and
\mathsf{tp}(T)
is

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable.

Proposition 45
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox that is consistent
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
. Then
\mathcal{A}\models Q(\vec{a})
iff

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\models\Phi_{Q}(\vec{a})
, for all
\vec{a}
in
\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof 9.1
.

Let
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. Assume
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. Let

\pi(x_{i})=a_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. By Lemma
44
,

\mathcal{A}
realizes some
(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
T
using
\pi
that avoids
q

such that
{\sf tp}(T)
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable. It is readily checked
that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\models\Psi_{T}(\pi_{1}(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
.
Thus,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\not\models\Phi_{Q}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})

Conversely, assume that
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\not\models\Phi_{Q}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. Take a
(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
T
that avoids
q
such that
{\sf tp}(T)
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}}\models\Psi_{T}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. Let
\pi(x_{i})=a_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. It is readily checked that
\mathcal{A}
realizes
T
using
\pi
.
Thus
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
, by
Lemma
44
.

This finishes the proof of Theorem
41
.

We now show that without the restriction on RIs adopted in
Theorem
41
, OMQCs from
(\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{UqfCQ})
are no longer FO-rewritable. In fact, we prove the
following, slightly stronger result by reduction from propositional
satisfiability.

Theorem 46
.

There is a
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
TBox with closed predicates
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})

such that ABox consistency w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
is
NP
-complete.

Proof 9.2
.

The proof is by reduction of the satisfiability problem for
propositional formulas in conjunctive normal form (CNF). Consider a
propositional formula in CNF
\varphi=c_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge c_{n}
, where each
c_{i}
is a disjunction of literals. We write

\ell\in c_{i}
if
\ell
is a disjunct in
c_{i}
.
Let
x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
be the propositional variables in

\varphi
. Define an ABox
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}
with individual names

c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
and
x_{i}^{\top}
,
x_{i}^{\bot}
,
x_{i}^{{\sf aux}}
for
1\leq i\leq m
, a concept name
A
, and role names

r,r^{\prime}
as the following set of assertions:

•

r(c_{i},x_{j}^{\top})
, for all
x_{j}\in c_{i}
and
1\leq i\leq n
;

•

r(c_{i},x_{j}^{\bot})
, for all
\neg x_{j}\in c_{i}
and

1\leq i\leq n
;

•

r^{\prime}(x_{j}^{\top},x_{j}^{\bot})
,
r^{\prime}(x_{j}^{\bot},x_{j}^{{\sf aux}})
, for
1\leq j\leq m
;

•

A(c_{i})
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Let
s
and
s^{\prime}
be additional role names and let

\mathcal{T}=\{s\sqsubseteq r,A\sqsubseteq\exists s,\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq\exists s^{\prime},s^{\prime}\sqsubseteq r^{\prime},\exists s^{\prime-}\sqcap\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq\bot\}.

Let
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\{A,r,r^{\prime}\}
. We show that
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}
is
consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
iff
\varphi
is
satisfiable. Assume first that
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}
is consistent
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
. Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of

\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}
that respects closed predicates

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Define a propositional valuation
v
by setting

v(x_{j})=1
if there exists
i
such that

(c_{i},x_{j}^{\top})\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
and set
v(x_{j})=0
if there exists

i
such that
(c_{i},x_{j}^{\bot})\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
. Observe that
v
is
well-defined since if
(c_{i},x_{j}^{\top})\in s^{\mathcal{I}},(c_{k},x_{j}^{\bot})\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
, then
(x_{j}^{\top},x_{j}^{\bot})\in s^{\prime\mathcal{I}}
and so

x_{j}^{\bot}\in(\exists s^{\prime-}\sqcap\exists s^{-})^{\mathcal{I}}
which
contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{I}
satisfies
\exists s^{\prime-}\sqcap\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq\bot
. Next observe that for every

c_{i}
there exists a disjunct
\ell\in c_{i}
such that

(c_{i},x_{j}^{\top})\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
if
\ell=x_{j}
and

(c_{i},x_{j}^{\bot})\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
if
\ell=\neg x_{j}
. Thus,

v(\varphi)=1
and
\varphi
is satisfiable.

Conversely, assume that
\varphi
is satisfiable and let
v
be an
assignment with
v(\varphi)=1
. Define an interpretation
\mathcal{I}
by expanding
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}}
as follows:

\displaystyle s^{\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(c_{i},x_{j}^{\top})\}\mid x_{j}\in c_{i},v(x_{j})=1,i\leq n\}\cup\{(c_{i},x_{j}^{\bot})\mid\neg x_{j}\in c_{i},v(x_{j})=0,i\leq n\}

\displaystyle s^{\prime\mathcal{I}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(x_{j}^{\top},x_{j}^{\bot})\mid v(x_{j})=1\}\cup\{(x_{j}^{\bot},x_{j}^{{\sf aux}})\mid v(x_{j})=0\}

It is readily checked that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
and

\mathcal{A}_{\varphi}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.

We close this section with noting that, for the case of
\mathcal{EL}
, quantifier-free queries are computationally no more
well-behaved than unrestricted queries. In fact, we have seen that OMQCs in
\mathcal{EL}
using dtUCQs can be
equivalently expressed using atomic database queries
A(x)
by adding CIs of the form
C_{q}\sqsubseteq A
to
the TBox.

10.
Conclusion

We have investigated the data complexity of ontology-mediated query
evaluation with closed predicates, focussing on a non-uniform
analysis. At the TBox level we have obtained
PTime
/
coNP
dichotomy
results for the lightweight DLs
\mathcal{EL}
and
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
. At the
query level, the situation is drastically different: there is provably
no
PTime
/
coNP
dichotomy for neither
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
nor
\mathcal{EL}

(unless
PTime
=
coNP
) and even without closing role names,
understanding the complexity of queries is as hard as understanding
the complexity of the generalized surjective constraint satisfaction
problems. We have also shown that by combining
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
with
quantifier-free database queries one obtains FO-rewritable queries and
that even for expressive DLs query evaluation is always in
coNP
. Many
challenging open questions remain.

Regarding the data complexity classification at TBox level, it is shown
in
[
45
]
that the dichotomy proof given for
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{EL}
does
not go through for the extension
\mathcal{ELI}
of
\mathcal{EL}
with inverse roles. In fact, in
contrast to
\text{DL-Lite}_{\mathcal{R}}
and
\mathcal{EL}
, there are
\mathcal{ELI}
TBoxes
with closed predicates
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
such that CQ evaluation w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
is in
PTime
,
but
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
and
(\mathcal{T},\emptyset)
are not CQ-inseparable on consistent ABoxes.
In particular, it remains open whether there is a
PTime
/
coNP
dichotomy for TBoxes with closed predicates
in
\mathcal{ELI}
. The same question remains open for
\mathcal{ALCHI}
TBoxes
(recall that there is a
PTime
/
coNP
dichotomy for for
\mathcal{ALCHI}
TBoxes without closed
predicates
[
47
,
33
]
) and for expressive Horn languages
such as Horn-
\mathcal{SHIQ}
. Also of interest are ontologies consisting of tuple-generating
dependencies (tgds) which generalizes both DL-Lite
R
and
\mathcal{EL}
.
In this case, however, the
coNP
upper bound established here for
\mathcal{ALCHI}
does not
hold, even for the moderate extension consisting of linear tgds
[
10
,
9
]
.

Regarding the data complexity classification at the OMQC level, it would be of interest to
consider
\text{$\text{DL-Lite}$ }_{\text{core}}
: it remains open whether there is a
PTime
/
coNP
dichotomy for the language
(\text{$\text{DL-Lite}$ }_{\text{core}},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{BUtCQ})

and whether Theorem
40
can be strengthened to this case.

Acknowledgments.
Frank Wolter was supported by EPSRC grant
EP/M012646/1. Carsten Lutz was supported by the ERC Consolidator
Grant 647289 CODA. We thank the anonymous reviewers for their suggestions and
comments.

Appendix A
Missing Proofs for Section
4

Lemma
5

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}}
-ABox,
\vec{a}
a tuple in
{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, and

Q=(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{A}},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},q)
a OMQC from
(\mathcal{ALC}\mathcal{HI},{\sf N_{C}}\cup{\sf N_{R}},\text{UCQ})
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{A}\models Q(\vec{a})
;

(2)

\mathcal{I}\models q(\vec{a})
for all forest-shaped models
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respect
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
and such that

•

the arity of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
is
|\mathcal{T}|
,

•

{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
is the set of roots of
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,

•

for every
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
and
\exists r.C\in{\sf cl}(\mathcal{T})
with
d\in(\exists r.C)^{\mathcal{I}}
, there exists
a\in{\sf Ind}(\mathcal{A})
with
(d,a)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
and
a\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
or
there exists a successor
d^{\prime}
of
d
in
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

such that
(d,d^{\prime})\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
and
d^{\prime}\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proof A.1
.

The implication from (1) to (2) is trivial.
For the converse direction, suppose
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q(\vec{a})
.
Then there is some model
\mathcal{J}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed predicates

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
such that
\mathcal{J}\not\models q(\vec{a})
. We construct, by
induction, a sequence of interpretations

\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots
. The domain of each
\mathcal{I}_{i}

consists of sequences of the form
d_{0}\cdot d_{1}\cdots d_{n}
,
where
d_{j}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
for all
j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
. We call such
sequences
paths
and denote the last element in a path
p
by

\mathsf{tail}(p)
, e.g.,
\mathsf{tail}(d_{0}\cdots d_{n})=d_{n}
.

We define
\mathcal{I}_{0}
as the restriction of
\mathcal{J}
to

\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{I}_{i}
is given. Let
p\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}

such that for some
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
and
\exists r.C\in{\sf cl}(\mathcal{T})
, we have

(\mathsf{tail}(p),e)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
and
e\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
and there is no
p^{\prime}\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
with

\mathsf{tail}(p^{\prime})=e^{\prime}
and
(p,p^{\prime})\in r^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
e^{\prime}\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
. Assume first that

e\not\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. We extend
\mathcal{I}_{i}
to
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
by setting

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\cup\{p\cdot e\}

\displaystyle s^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\cup\{(p,p\cdot e)\mid(\mathsf{tail}(p),e)\in s^{\mathcal{J}}\}\cup\{(p\cdot e,p)\mid(e,\mathsf{tail}(p))\in s^{\mathcal{J}}\}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\cup\{p\cdot e\mid e\in A^{\mathcal{J}}\}

for all role names
s
and concept names
A
. Suppose now that
e=a
for some
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. In this case, we
extend
\mathcal{I}_{i}
to
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
by adding the tuple
(p,e)
to

s^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, for every role
s
such that

(\mathsf{tail}(p),e)\in s^{\mathcal{J}}
.

We assume that the above construction is
fair
in the sense that
if the conditions of the inductive step are satisfied for some

p\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
,
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
, and
\exists r.C\in{\sf cl}(\mathcal{T})
, with
i\geq 0
,
then there is some
j>i
such that the inductive step is applied to

p
,
e
, and
\exists r.C
.

Now we define the interpretation
\mathcal{I}
as the limit of the sequence
\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{1},\ldots
:

•

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
;

•

P^{\mathcal{I}}=\bigcup_{i\geq 0}P^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, for all

P\in\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{C}}\cup\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{R}}
.

It is clear that
\mathcal{I}
is a forest-shaped interpretation with

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
a
|\mathcal{T}|
-ary forest having precisely

\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
as its roots. That
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
is
an easy consequence of the facts that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of

\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is the restriction of
\mathcal{J}
to

\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, and
\mathcal{I}
is an extension of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
. That

\mathcal{I}
respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
is by definition.
We now show that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
. The following is easily proved by structural induction.

Claim
.

For all
p\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and
C\in{\sf cl}(\mathcal{T})
,
p\in C^{\mathcal{I}}\text{ iff }\mathsf{tail}(p)\in C^{\mathcal{J}}
.

The fact that
\mathcal{J}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
now implies that

\mathcal{I}
is a model of every CI in
\mathcal{T}
. That

\mathcal{I}
is a model of every RI in
\mathcal{T}
follows by construction.
Hence we conclude that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
.

Finally, to show that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\vec{a})
, observe that

h=\{p\mapsto\mathsf{tail}(p)\mid p\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\}
is a homomorphism from
\mathcal{I}
to

\mathcal{J}
preserving
{\sf N_{I}}
. Thus,
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\vec{a})
follows from Lemma
4

and
\mathcal{J}\not\models q(\vec{a})
.

Lemma 47
.

The interpretation
\mathcal{I}
defined in the proof of Lemma
6
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}

that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\vec{a})
.

Proof A.2
.

The following conditions follow directly from the construction of
\mathcal{I}
and the conditions on mosaics:

•

\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{A}
;

•

\mathcal{I}
is a model of every RI in
\mathcal{T}
;

•

P^{\mathcal{I}}=\{\vec{a}\mid P(\vec{a})\in\mathcal{A}\}
, for all predicates
P\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of every concept
inclusion in
\mathcal{T}
. Define for every
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, a

\mathcal{T}
-type
t_{d}
as follows.

•

if
d\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, then let
t_{d}=\tau(d)
for some

(\mathcal{J},\tau)\in M
;

•

if
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, then
t_{d}=\tau_{d}(d)
.

To prove that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
it is now sufficient
to show the following: for all
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
and

C\in\mathsf{cl}(\mathcal{T})
,
d\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
C\in t_{d}
. The proof is
by structural induction.

Let
C=A\in\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{C}}
. If
d\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, let

(\mathcal{J},\tau)
be any mosaic in
M
; and if

d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, then let

(\mathcal{J},\tau)=(\mathcal{I}_{d},\tau_{d})
. We have (i)
d\in B^{\mathcal{I}}
iff

d\in B^{\mathcal{J}}
for all
B\in\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{C}}\cap\mathsf{cl}(\mathcal{T})
and
(ii)
\tau(d)=t_{d}
. But then
d\in A^{\mathcal{I}}
iff
d\in A^{\mathcal{J}}
(by
(i)) iff
A\in\tau(d)
(by the definition of a mosaic) iff
A\in t_{d}
(by (ii)).

The boolean cases follow easily by the induction hypothesis and
the fact that
t_{d}
is a
\mathcal{T}
-type.

Let
C=\exists r.D
. For the direction from left to right,
suppose
d\in(\exists r.D)^{\mathcal{I}}
. Then there is some

e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
and
e\in D^{\mathcal{I}}
. If

d,e\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, let
(\mathcal{J},\tau)
be any mosaic in
M
;
if
d,e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, let

(\mathcal{J},\tau)=(\mathcal{I}_{d^{\prime}},\tau_{d^{\prime}})
, where
d^{\prime}
is the
element of
\{d,e\}
that has the smaller depth in

\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
; otherwise let

(\mathcal{J},\tau)=(\mathcal{I}_{d^{\prime}},\tau_{d^{\prime}})
, where
d^{\prime}
is the only
element of
(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A}))\cap\{d,e\}
. Observe
that
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
,
\tau(d)=t_{d}
, and
\tau(e)=t_{e}
. By

(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}
and the definition of a mosaic, we obtain

\tau(d)\rightsquigarrow_{r}\tau(e)
and by the induction hypothesis
and
\tau(e)=t_{e}
, we obtain
D\in\tau(e)
. But then

\exists r.D\in\tau(d)
and thus,
\exists r.D\in t_{d}
,
which is what we wanted to show.

For the direction from right to left, suppose
\exists r.D\in t_{d}
. We distinguish between
d\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
or not. For the
former case, we find by the coherency of
M
a
(\mathcal{J},\tau)\in M
such that for some
e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{J}}
we have
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{J}}

and
C\in\tau(e)
; for the latter case, we have by the definition
of a mosaic and
|q|\geq 1
that there is some

e\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{d}}
with
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}_{d}}
and

C\in\tau_{d}(e)
. In both cases, we have by the construction of

\mathcal{I}
that
(d,e)\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
and by definition that
C\in t_{e}
. By the latter, the induction hypothesis yields

e\in C^{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence,
d\in(\exists r.D)^{\mathcal{I}}
, as required.

It remains to show that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\vec{a})
. Assume
\vec{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
. For a proof by contradiction,
suppose that
\mathcal{I}\models q(\vec{a})
. Then there is a disjunct

\exists\vec{y}\varphi(\vec{x},\vec{y})
of
q
with
\vec{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\varphi
a conjunction of
atoms such that there is an assignment
\pi
mapping the variables
\vec{x}\cup\vec{y}
of
\varphi
to
\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}

with
\pi(x_{i})=a_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
\mathcal{I}\models_{\pi}\varphi
. Let
F=\{\pi(x)\mid\pi(x)\not\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\}
.
As
\mathcal{I}
is forest-shaped there are
T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}
with
F=T_{1}\cup\cdots\cup T_{m}

such that
T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}
are maximal and pairwise disjoint trees in
F
.
Fix an
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
. Let
d
be the root of
T_{i}
. By the
construction of
\mathcal{I}
, there is an isomorphism
f_{i}
trom
(\mathcal{I}_{d},\tau_{d})
to some
(\mathcal{J},\tau)\in M
.
Let
\pi_{i}
be the restriction of
\pi
to those variables that are mapped to
T_{i}
, and let
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}
be the restriction of
\pi

to those variables that are mapped to
\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. Define

\pi_{i}^{\prime}=f_{i}\circ\pi_{i}
and then

\pi^{\prime}=\bigcup^{m}_{i=1}\pi_{i}^{\prime}\cup\pi_{\mathcal{A}}.

\pi^{\prime}
is an assignment in
\biguplus_{(\mathcal{J},\tau)\in M}\mathcal{J}
with
\pi^{\prime}(x_{i})=a_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n

such that
\biguplus_{(\mathcal{J},\tau)\in M}\mathcal{J}\models_{\pi^{\prime}}\varphi
, and so we have derived a contradiction.

Appendix B
Missing Proofs for Section
9

Lemma
42

A
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox
\mathcal{A}
is consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
iff

(1)

\mathcal{A}
has a
\mathcal{T}
-decoration
f
whose image is a

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable
\mathcal{T}
-typing and

(2)

if
s(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
, and
\mathsf{sig}(s\sqsubseteq r)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
then
r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
.

Proof B.1
.

(\Rightarrow)
Let
\mathcal{I}
be a model of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}
that
respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. For each
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
,
let
t_{\mathcal{I}}^{d}=\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid d\in B^{\mathcal{I}}\}
and let

T_{\mathcal{I}}=\{t_{\mathcal{I}}^{a}\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\}
. We next show that
the
\mathcal{T}
-typing
T_{\mathcal{I}}
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable. Let
t_{\mathcal{I}}^{a},r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
be a

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating path in
T_{\mathcal{I}}
. Using
\mathcal{I}
, we find a
mapping
g:\{0,\ldots,n\}\rightarrow\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that
g(0)=a
and
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, we have

(a)

(g(i-1),g(i))\in r_{i}^{\mathcal{I}}
,

(b)

g(i)\in B^{\mathcal{I}}
for all
B\in\mathsf{sub}(\mathcal{T})
with

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r^{-}_{i}\sqsubseteq B
.

By definition of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating paths, there is some

B^{\star}\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
with
\mathsf{sig}(B^{\star})\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}

such that
\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B^{\star}
. By
Point (b), we obtain
g(n)\in{B^{\star}}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Since
\mathcal{I}
is a model
of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
, we
have
g(n)=b
for some
b\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. By Point (b),

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B
implies
B\in t_{\mathcal{I}}^{b}
for any
B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
. Thus,
T_{\mathcal{I}}
is

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable. Let
f(a)=t^{a}_{\mathcal{I}}
for all

a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
. It is clear that
f
is a
\mathcal{T}
-decoration of

\mathcal{A}
. The image of
f
is
T_{\mathcal{I}}
, thus a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable

\mathcal{T}
-typing. Hence we conclude that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Point (1). Point (2) holds by the fact that
\mathcal{I}
is a
model of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed predicates

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.

(\Leftarrow)
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
satisfies Points (1) and (2)
and let
f
be a
\mathcal{T}
-decoration of
\mathcal{A}
whose image
T
is a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable
\mathcal{T}
-typing.
Our goal is to construct a model
\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed
predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
as the limit of a sequence of interpretations

\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots
. The domains of these interpretations consist
of the individual names from
\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
and of paths in
T
that
are not
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating. The construction will ensure that
for all
i
, we have

(a)

for all
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, we have
t^{a}_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\subseteq f(a)
;

(b)

for all
p\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
, if
p=t,r_{1}\dots,r_{n}
, then we have

t^{p}_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\subseteq\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}
.

Define
\mathcal{I}_{0}=(\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}},\cdot^{\mathcal{I}_{0}})
where

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle r^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{(a,b)\mid s(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and
}\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{a\mid A\in f(a)\}

To construct
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
from

\mathcal{I}_{i}
, choose
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
\exists s\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})

such that
\mathsf{sig}(s)\cap\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{T}\models\bigsqcap t^{d}_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\sqsubseteq\exists s
and there is no

(d,e)\in s^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
. Let
q=f(a),s
if
d=a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})

and
q=d,s
otherwise. Using Conditions (a) and (b), it is easy to
verify that
q
is a path in
T
. If
q
is not

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating, then define
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\uplus\{q\}

\displaystyle r^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}r^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\cup\{(d,q)\}&\text{if }\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\\
r^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\cup\{q\}&\text{if }\mathcal{T}\models\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq A\\
A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
q
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating, then by the fact that
T
is

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable, there is some
t\in T
such that

\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}\subseteq t
. We find a
b\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
with

t=f(b)
. Define
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}

\displaystyle r^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}r^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\cup\{(d,b)\}&\text{if }\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r\\
r^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}.

Assume that the choice of
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
\exists s\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
is fair so that every possible combination of

d
and
\exists s
is eventually chosen. Let

\mathcal{I}
be the limit of the sequence
\mathcal{I}_{0},\mathcal{I}_{1},\dots
(cf. the proof of Lemma
5
).
We claim that
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that
respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. By definition of
\mathcal{I}_{0}

and of
\mathcal{T}
-decorations, it is straightforward to see that

\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{A}
. Moreover, the RIs in
\mathcal{T}
are
clearly satisfied. To show that the CIs are
satisfied as well, it is straightforward to first establish the
following strengthenings of Conditions (a) and (b) above (details
omitted):

(a
′
)

for all
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, we have
t^{a}_{\mathcal{I}}=f(a)
;

(b
′
)

for all
p\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
, if
p=t,r_{1}\dots,r_{n}
, then

t^{p}_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}=\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}
.

Let
a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
,
a\in B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and
B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
(or
B_{1}\sqsubseteq\neg B_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
). Then by
Condition
(a^{\prime})
and since
f(a)
is a
\mathcal{T}
-type, we have

a\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
(resp.
a\not\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
). Now let

d=t,r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}
be a path. First suppose
d\in B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
and

B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
. By Condition (b
′
), we conclude that

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B_{1}
. Since

B_{1}\sqsubseteq B_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
, it follows that

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B_{2}
and thus again by
the property above,
d\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
. Finally, suppose

d\in B_{1}^{\mathcal{I}}
and
B_{1}\sqsubseteq\neg B_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
. By
Condition (b
′
) and
B_{1}\sqsubseteq\neg B_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
, we conclude

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq\neg B_{2}
. For a proof
by contradiction assume that
d\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
and thus

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq B_{2}
and we already have

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}^{-}\sqsubseteq\neg B_{2}
. Hence

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r^{-}_{n}\sqsubseteq\bot
. But then

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{n}\sqsubseteq\bot
. It follows that

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{1}\sqsubseteq\bot
. This implies in
particular
\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{1}\sqsubseteq\exists r_{1}

and

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{1}\sqsubseteq\neg\exists r_{1}
. By
definition we have
\exists r_{1}\in f(a)
and by

\mathcal{T}\models\exists r_{1}\sqsubseteq\neg\exists r_{1}
and
the fact that
f(a)
is a
\mathcal{T}
-type, we obtain

\exists r_{1}\not\in f(a)
, i.e., a contradiction. Hence

d\not\in B_{2}^{\mathcal{I}}
which finishes the proof that

\mathcal{I}\models\mathcal{T}
.

What remains to be shown are the following properties:

•

for all
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
A^{\mathcal{I}}=\{a\mid A(a)\in\mathcal{A}\}
;

•

for all
r\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
,
r^{\mathcal{I}}=\{(a,b)\mid r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}\}
.

We show for each
i\geq 0
that
\mathcal{I}_{i}
satisfies the
properties above.

Suppose
i=0
. First let
A(a)\in\mathcal{A}
with
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Then

a\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
by definition of
\mathcal{I}_{0}
. For the other
direction, let
a\in A^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
for an
A\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Then
A\in f(a)
. The definition of
\mathcal{T}
-decorations yields
A\in t^{a}_{\mathcal{A}}
,
and thus
A(a)\in\mathcal{A}
. Now let
r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
with

r\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Then
(a,b)\in r^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
by definition of

\mathcal{I}_{0}
. For the other direction, let
(a,b)\in r^{\mathcal{I}_{0}}
for
some
r\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Then there is some role
s
such that

s(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
. By the adopted
restriction on the allowed RIs, it follows that

\mathsf{sig}(s)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. This yields
r(a,b)\in\mathcal{A}
since

\mathcal{A}
satisfies Point (2) of Lemma
42
.

For
i>0
, we show that the extension of
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-predicates is
not modified when constructing
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
from

\mathcal{I}_{i}
. Indeed, assume that
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
was obtained from

\mathcal{I}_{i}
by choosing
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
\exists s\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
and let
q=f(a),s
if
d=a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
and

q=d,s
otherwise. Then
\mathsf{sig}(s)\cap\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\emptyset
and
by the restriction on RIs,
\mathsf{sig}(r)\cap\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\emptyset
for any role
r
with
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
. Consequently, none of the role names modified in the
construction of
\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
is from
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
(no matter whether

q
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating or not). In the case where
q
is

\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating, there is nothing else to show. If
q
is
not
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-participating, then each concept name
A
with
\mathcal{T}\models\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq A
is not from
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Thus also
none of the concept names modified in the construction of

\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
is from
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
.

Lemma
44

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-ABox consistent
w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
. Then
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
iff
\mathcal{A}
realizes some

(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
T
using
\pi
that avoids
q
and such that

\mathsf{tp}(T)
is
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-realizable.

Proof B.2
.

The proof is a modification of the proof of
Lemma
42
. We only sketch the differences.

(
\Rightarrow
) Let
\mathcal{A}\not\models Q(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
. We start with a model
\mathcal{I}
of

\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
such
that
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
. Read off a

(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing

T_{\mathcal{I}}=(\sim,f_{0},\Gamma,\Delta)

from
\mathcal{I}
by setting

•

x_{i}\sim x_{j}
iff
\pi(x_{i})=\pi(x_{j})
;

•

f_{0}(x_{i})=t^{\pi(x_{i})}_{\mathcal{I}}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
;

•

\Gamma=\{t^{a}_{\mathcal{I}}\mid a\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\}\setminus\{\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n})\}
;

•

\Delta=\{r(x_{i},x_{j})\mid r\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}},r(\pi(x_{i}),\pi(x_{j}))\not\in\mathcal{A}\}
.

We show that
T_{\mathcal{I}}
avoids
q=\bigvee_{i\in I}q_{i}
. Since
\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
we find for every
i\in I
an atom
\alpha_{i}
in
q_{i}
such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models\alpha_{i}(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
. We show that
T_{\mathcal{I}}

avoids
X=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
. We distinguish the following
cases:

•

Let
A(x)\in X
. Then
A\not\in t^{\pi(x)}_{\mathcal{I}}
and so

A\not\in f_{0}(x)
, as required.

•

Let
\exists s\in f_{0}(x)
. Then
\exists s\in t^{\pi(x)}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Thus, there exists
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}
such that

(\pi(x),d)\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
. If
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
, then
\mathsf{sig}(B)\cap\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\emptyset
for all

B\in t^{d}_{\mathcal{I}}
. Thus (i) holds. If
d\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\setminus\{\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n})\}
, then (ii)
holds. Now assume that
d=\pi(y)
for some
y\in\{\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n})\}
. Then
y
satisfies the
conditions for (iii).

•

Let
r(x,y)\in X
. Then
(\pi(x),\pi(y))\not\in r^{\mathcal{I}}
. Hence

(\pi(x),\pi(y))\not\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
for any
s\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
with

\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
. Thus
s(x,y)\in\Delta
for any such

s
. Moreover,
(\pi(y),\pi(x))\not\in s^{\mathcal{I}}
for any
s\in\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
with
\mathcal{T}\models s^{-}\sqsubseteq r
. Thus
s(y,x)\in\Delta
for any such
s
.

(
\Leftarrow
) Assume that a
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
-Abox
\mathcal{A}
that is
consistent w.r.t.
(\mathcal{T},\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}})
realizes some

(\mathcal{T},q)
-typing
T=(\sim,f_{0},\Gamma,\Delta)
using
\pi
that avoids

q
. Assume
f
is a
\mathcal{T},q
-decoration of
\mathcal{A}
that realizes
T

using
\pi
. Let
X=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
with
\alpha_{i}
in
q_{i}

such that
T
avoids
X
using
\pi
. We construct a model

\mathcal{I}
of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{T}
that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}

such that
\mathcal{I}\not\models\alpha_{i}[\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n})]

for
i\in I
. We build
\mathcal{I}
as in the proof of Lemma
42

based on
\mathsf{tp}(T)
. Some care is required in the construction of

\mathcal{I}_{i+1}
. Assume
\mathcal{I}_{i}
has been constructed. Choose
d\in\Delta^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
and
\exists s\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})
such that

\mathsf{sig}(s)\cap\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{T}\models\bigsqcap t^{d}_{\mathcal{I}_{i}}\sqsubseteq\exists s
and there is no
(d,e)\in s^{\mathcal{I}_{i}}
. If
d\not\in\{\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n})\}
or

\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}

does not contain a
B
with
\mathsf{sig}(B)\subseteq\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
proceed as
in the proof of Lemma
42
. Now assume that
d=\pi(x)
. In
the proof of Lemma
42
we chose an
arbitrary

b\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})
with
\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}\subseteq t
and
t=f(b)
and added
(a,b)
to

r^{\mathcal{I}_{i+1}}
whenever
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq r
. Since we want
to refute all atoms
\alpha_{i}(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
with

i\in I
, we now have to choose
b
more carefully. If there exists

b\in\mathsf{Ind}(\mathcal{A})\setminus\{\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n})\}
with

\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}\subseteq t
and
t=f(b)
, then we choose such a
b
and proceed as in
Lemma
42
. Otherwise, since
f
is a
\mathcal{T},q
-decoration of

\mathcal{A}
that realizes
T
using
\pi
and avoids
X
, there is
y
such
that
\{B\in\mathsf{con}(\mathcal{T})\mid\mathcal{T}\models\exists s^{-}\sqsubseteq B\}\subseteq f_{0}(y)
such that there is no
\alpha_{i}\in X
of the
form
t(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
or
t(y^{\prime},x^{\prime})
with
x^{\prime}\sim x
and
y^{\prime}\sim y
such
that
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq t
or
\mathcal{T}\models s\sqsubseteq t^{-}
,
respectively. We set
b=\pi(y)
and proceed as in the proof of
Lemma
42
.

The resulting interpretation
\mathcal{I}
is a model of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{A}

that respects closed predicates
\Sigma_{\mathsf{C}}
. Moreover
\mathcal{I}\not\models\alpha_{i}(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
for all
i\in I
. Thus,

\mathcal{I}\not\models q(\pi(x_{1}),\ldots,\pi(x_{n}))
, as required.
